## P3 - `UpdateMgmtIntf` never applies the new `ipv6 nd ra suppress all` for existing labs

**Location:** [nodes/iol/iol.go:719-732](https://github.com/srl-labs/containerlab/blob/9323d7eff382af82e90367ce52a25c4312b7e68e/nodes/iol/iol.go#L719-L732)

**Problem:** The new `ipv6 nd ra suppress all` line only exists in the first-boot bootstrap template &#40;[`nodes/iol/iol.cfg.tmpl`](https://github.com/srl-labs/containerlab/blob/9323d7eff382af82e90367ce52a25c4312b7e68e/nodes/iol/iol.cfg.tmpl#L1-L1) line 30&#41;. The `PostDeploy` -&#62; `UpdateMgmtIntf` path exists to push idempotent management commands to nodes whose configuration persists in NVRAM, and it already re-sends `no ipv6 address` and `ipv6 address` for convergence, but it does not include the RA suppression command.

**Trigger:** Deploy an IOL lab with a pre-containerlab-change release, save the configuration to NVRAM with `write memory` as described in [`docs/manual/kinds/cisco_iol.md`](https://github.com/srl-labs/containerlab/blob/9323d7eff382af82e90367ce52a25c4312b7e68e/docs/manual/kinds/cisco_iol.md#L1-L1), then upgrade containerlab and redeploy. The saved NVRAM configuration wins over the regenerated `boot_config.txt`, and `UpdateMgmtIntf` only re-pushes addressing and routes.

**Impact:** Pre-existing labs keep emitting IPv6 Router Advertisements on the management network, which is the exact problem this PR intends to stop, with no error or indication. New labs are fixed; upgraded ones silently are not.

**Suggested correction:** Append `ipv6 nd ra suppress all` to the `mgmt_str` command sequence in `UpdateMgmtIntf` right after the `ipv6 address` push. The command is interface-static and idempotent, matching the existing &#39;safe to re-run&#39; command list, and converges existing labs at the shared root cause.


## P4 - RA suppression default change is missing from release notes and the IOL kind documentation

**Location:** [nodes/iol/iol.cfg.tmpl:30-30](https://github.com/srl-labs/containerlab/blob/9323d7eff382af82e90367ce52a25c4312b7e68e/nodes/iol/iol.cfg.tmpl#L30-L30)

**Problem:** Suppressing Router Advertisements changes observable default behavior of every IOL node&#39;s management interface, but it is not reflected in the &#39;basic configuration which configures the following&#39; list in [`docs/manual/kinds/cisco_iol.md`](https://github.com/srl-labs/containerlab/blob/9323d7eff382af82e90367ce52a25c4312b7e68e/docs/manual/kinds/cisco_iol.md#L1-L1) &#40;lines 152-158&#41; nor in any release note; the latest [`docs/rn/0.83.md`](https://github.com/srl-labs/containerlab/blob/9323d7eff382af82e90367ce52a25c4312b7e68e/docs/rn/0.83.md#L1-L1) is already published and no unreleased note exists.

**Trigger:** A user reading the IOL documentation or release notes cannot learn that RAs are now suppressed on the management interface, and someone debugging SLAAC or RA-related behavior on the management network would not connect it to this default.

**Impact:** Low&#58; purely informational, but the documentation enumeration is otherwise kept in sync with the template and the repository convention records kind-level default changes in release notes.

**Suggested correction:** Add one bullet to the basic-configuration list in [`docs/manual/kinds/cisco_iol.md`](https://github.com/srl-labs/containerlab/blob/9323d7eff382af82e90367ce52a25c4312b7e68e/docs/manual/kinds/cisco_iol.md#L1-L1) &#40;for example, &#39;Router Advertisements are suppressed on the management interface.&#39;&#41; and a one-line entry in the next unreleased release note&#39;s `Miscellaneous` section once it exists.


## Coverage

- The diff touches exactly one file, [`nodes/iol/iol.cfg.tmpl`](https://github.com/srl-labs/containerlab/blob/9323d7eff382af82e90367ce52a25c4312b7e68e/nodes/iol/iol.cfg.tmpl#L1-L1); both base and head versions were read in full and the added line is syntactically and positionally correct inside the management interface block, rendering identically for L2 and routed node types.
- Traced the template consumer path&#58; &#96;//go&#58;embed [`iol.cfg.tmpl`](https://github.com/srl-labs/containerlab/blob/9323d7eff382af82e90367ce52a25c4312b7e68e/nodes/iol/iol.cfg.tmpl#L1-L1) -&#62; `GenBootConfig` -&#62; `boot_config.txt` written on every `PostDeploy`, and the parallel postdeploy path `UpdateMgmtIntf`, which is the basis for the P3 finding.
- Checked [`nodes/iol/iol_test.go`](https://github.com/srl-labs/containerlab/blob/9323d7eff382af82e90367ce52a25c4312b7e68e/nodes/iol/iol_test.go#L1-L1) &#40;covers `UpdateMgmtIntf` timeout and L2 detection; no template-content assertions&#41; and the `tests/10-basic-cisco_iol` Robot suite &#40;no assertions on RA suppression, boot config, or NVRAM content&#41;. A rendered-config assertion would be a possible follow-up but is not required for a one-line template change.
- No topology schema implications&#58; this is an internal node-kind template change, so no [`schemas/clab.schema.json`](https://github.com/srl-labs/containerlab/blob/9323d7eff382af82e90367ce52a25c4312b7e68e/schemas/clab.schema.json#L1-L1) edit is expected.
- Out of scope of the PR, noted without a finding&#58; data interfaces configured with IPv6 addresses &#40;template lines 37-39&#41; also send RAs on lab links; the PR scopes suppression to the management interface only, which looks intentional.
- This was a source-only review&#58; no tests, builds, or deployments were executed, and no runtime verification of the `ipv6 nd ra suppress all` command against a specific IOL image was possible; command validity is assessed from standard IOS/IOS-XE interface configuration.

<type>file</type>
<content>
1: ---
2: description: Behavioral guidelines to reduce common LLM coding mistakes. Use when writing, reviewing, or refactoring code to avoid overcomplication, make surgical changes, surface assumptions, and define verifiable success criteria.
3: alwaysApply: true
4: ---
5: 
6: # Karpathy behavioral guidelines
7: 
8: Behavioral guidelines to reduce common LLM coding mistakes. Merge with project-specific instructions as needed.
9: 
10: **Tradeoff:** These guidelines bias toward caution over speed. For trivial tasks, use judgment.
11: 
12: ## 1. Think Before Coding
13: 
14: **Don't assume. Don't hide confusion. Surface tradeoffs.**
15: 
16: Before implementing:
17: 
18: - State your assumptions explicitly. If uncertain, ask.
19: - If multiple interpretations exist, present them - don't pick silently.
20: - If a simpler approach exists, say so. Push back when warranted.
21: - If something is unclear, stop. Name what's confusing. Ask.
22: 
23: ## 2. Simplicity First
24: 
25: **Minimum code that solves the problem. Nothing speculative.**
26: 
27: - No features beyond what was asked.
28: - No abstractions for single-use code.
29: - No "flexibility" or "configurability" that wasn't requested.
30: - No error handling for impossible scenarios.
31: - If you write 200 lines and it could be 50, rewrite it.
32: 
33: Ask yourself: "Would a senior engineer say this is overcomplicated?" If yes, simplify.
34: 
35: ## 3. Surgical Changes
36: 
37: **Touch only what you must. Clean up only your own mess.**
38: 
39: When editing existing code:
40: 
41: - Don't "improve" adjacent code, comments, or formatting.
42: - Don't refactor things that aren't broken.
43: - Match existing style, even if you'd do it differently.
44: - If you notice unrelated dead code, mention it - don't delete it.
45: 
46: When your changes create orphans:
47: 
48: - Remove imports/variables/functions that YOUR changes made unused.
49: - Don't remove pre-existing dead code unless asked.
50: 
51: The test: Every changed line should trace directly to the user's request.
52: 
53: ## 4. Goal-Driven Execution
54: 
55: **Define success criteria. Loop until verified.**
56: 
57: Transform tasks into verifiable goals:
58: 
59: - "Add validation" → "Write tests for invalid inputs, then make them pass"
60: - "Fix the bug" → "Write a test that reproduces it, then make it pass"
61: - "Refactor X" → "Ensure tests pass before and after"
62: 
63: For multi-step tasks, state a brief plan:
64: 
65: ```
66: 1. [Step] → verify: [check]
67: 2. [Step] → verify: [check]
68: 3. [Step] → verify: [check]
69: ```
70: 
71: Strong success criteria let you loop independently. Weak criteria ("make it work") require constant clarification.
72: 
73: ---
74: 
75: **These guidelines are working if:** fewer unnecessary changes in diffs, fewer rewrites due to overcomplication, and clarifying questions come before implementation rather than after mistakes.

(End of file - total 75 lines)
</content>

[Step] → verify: [check]\n67: 2. [Step] → verify: [check]\n68: 3. [Step] → verify: [check]\n69: ```\n70: \n71: Strong success criteria let you loop independently. Weak criteria (\"make it work\") require constant clarification.\n72: \n73: ---\n74: \n75: **These guidelines are working if:** fewer unnecessary changes in diffs, fewer rewrites due to overcomplication, and clarifying questions come before implementation rather than after mistakes.\n\n(End of file - total 75 lines)\n</content>",

        "text": "---\ndescription: Behavioral guidelines to reduce common LLM coding mistakes. Use when writing, reviewing, or refactoring code to avoid overcomplication, make surgical changes, surface assumptions, and define verifiable success criteria.\nalwaysApply: true\n---\n\n# Karpathy behavioral guidelines\n\nBehavioral guidelines to reduce common LLM coding mistakes. Merge with project-specific instructions as needed.\n\n**Tradeoff:** These guidelines bias toward caution over speed. For trivial tasks, use judgment.\n\n## 1. Think Before Coding\n\n**Don't assume. Don't hide confusion. Surface tradeoffs.**\n\nBefore implementing:\n\n- State your assumptions explicitly. If uncertain, ask.\n- If multiple interpretations exist, present them - don't pick silently.\n- If a simpler approach exists, say so. Push back when warranted.\n- If something is unclear, stop. Name what's confusing. Ask.\n\n## 2. Simplicity First\n\n**Minimum code that solves the problem. Nothing speculative.**\n\n- No features beyond what was asked.\n- No abstractions for single-use code.\n- No \"flexibility\" or \"configurability\" that wasn't requested.\n- No error handling for impossible scenarios.\n- If you write 200 lines and it could be 50, rewrite it.\n\nAsk yourself: \"Would a senior engineer say this is overcomplicated?\" If yes, simplify.\n\n## 3. Surgical Changes\n\n**Touch only what you must. Clean up only your own mess.**\n\nWhen editing existing code:\n\n- Don't \"improve\" adjacent code, comments, or formatting.\n- Don't refactor things that aren't broken.\n- Match existing style, even if you'd do it differently.\n- If you notice unrelated dead code, mention it - don't delete it.\n\nWhen your changes create orphans:\n\n- Remove imports/variables/functions that YOUR changes made unused.\n- Don't remove pre-existing dead code unless asked.\n\nThe test: Every changed line should trace directly to the user's request.\n\n## 4. Goal-Driven Execution\n\n**Define success criteria. Loop until verified.**\n\nTransform tasks into verifiable goals:\n\n- \"Add validation\" → \"Write tests for invalid inputs, then make them pass\"\n- \"Fix the bug\" → \"Write a test that reproduces it, then make it pass\"\n- \"Refactor X\" → \"Ensure tests pass before and after\"\n\nFor multi-step tasks, state a brief plan:\n\n```\n1. [Step] → verify: [check]\n2. [Step] → verify: [check]\n3. [Step] → verify: [check]\n```\n\nStrong success criteria let you loop independently. Weak criteria (\"make it work\") require constant clarification.\n\n---\n\n**These guidelines are working if:** fewer unnecessary changes in diffs, fewer rewrites due to overcomplication, and clarifying questions come before implementation rather than after mistakes.",

Output

<path>/source/standards/ponytail.mdc</path>
<type>file</type>
<content>
1: ---
2: description: Ponytail, lazy senior dev mode. Always pick the simplest solution that works.
3: globs:
4: alwaysApply: true
5: ---
6: 
7: # Ponytail, lazy senior dev mode
8: 
9: You are a lazy senior developer. Lazy means efficient, not careless. The best code is the code never written.
10: 
11: Before writing any code, stop at the first rung that holds:
12: 
13: 1. Does this need to be built at all? (YAGNI)
14: 2. Does it already exist in this codebase? Reuse the helper, util, or pattern that's already here, don't re-write it.
15: 3. Does the standard library already do this? Use it.
16: 4. Does a native platform feature cover it? Use it.
17: 5. Does an already-installed dependency solve it? Use it.
18: 6. Can this be one line? Make it one line.
19: 7. Only then: write the minimum code that works.
20: 
21: The ladder runs after you understand the problem, not instead of it: read the task and the code it touches, trace the real flow end to end, then climb.
22: 
23: Bug fix = root cause, not symptom: a report names a symptom. Grep every caller of the function you touch and fix the shared function once — one guard there is a smaller diff than one per caller, and patching only the path the ticket names leaves a sibling caller still broken.
24: 
25: Rules:
26: 
27: - No abstractions that weren't explicitly requested.
28: - No new dependency if it can be avoided.
29: - No boilerplate nobody asked for.
30: - Deletion over addition. Boring over clever. Fewest files possible.
31: - Shortest working diff wins, but only once you understand the problem. The smallest change in the wrong place isn't lazy, it's a second bug.
32: - Question complex requests: "Do you actually need X, or does Y cover it?"
33: - Pick the edge-case-correct option when two stdlib approaches are the same size, lazy means less code, not the flimsier algorithm.
34: - Mark deliberate simplifications that cut a real corner with a known ceiling (global lock, O(n²) scan, naive heuristic) with a `ponytail:` comment naming the ceiling and upgrade path.
35: 
36: Not lazy about: understanding the problem (read it fully and trace the real flow before picking a rung, a small diff you don't understand is just laziness dressed up as efficiency), input validation at trust boundaries, error handling that prevents data loss, security, accessibility, the calibration real hardware needs (the platform is never the spec ideal, a clock drifts, a sensor reads off), anything explicitly requested. Lazy code without its check is unfinished: non-trivial logic leaves ONE runnable check behind, the smallest thing that fails if the logic breaks (an assert-based demo/self-check or one small test file; no frameworks, no fixtures). Trivial one-liners need no test.

(End of file - total 36 lines)
</content>

65: // Register registers the node in the NodeRegistry.
66: func Register(r *clabnodes.NodeRegistry) {

84: // KindSpecificConfig is the cisco_iol kind-specific config, set as keys on the node definition.
85: type KindSpecificConfig struct {
86: 	// PidOffset shifts the auto-assigned IOL PID, e.g. to keep IDs unique across labs.
87: 	PidOffset *int `yaml:"pid-offset,omitempty" json:"pid-offset,omitempty"`
88: 	// MgmtIntf is the management interface, e.g. Ethernet0/1. Defaults to Ethernet0/0.
89: 	MgmtIntf string `yaml:"mgmt-intf,omitempty" json:"mgmt-intf,omitempty"`
90: 	// BootstrapConfig replaces the default startup configuration with a file, or disables it
91: 	// when set to "none".
92: 	BootstrapConfig string `yaml:"bootstrap-config,omitempty" json:"bootstrap-config,omitempty"`
93: }

115: func (n *iol) kindSpecificCfg() *KindSpecificConfig { return kindSpecificConfig.Of(n.Cfg) }

117: func (n *iol) Init(cfg *clabtypes.NodeConfig, opts ...clabnodes.NodeOption) error {
118: 	// Init DefaultNode
119: 	n.DefaultNode = *clabnodes.NewDefaultNode(n)
120: 	n.firstBoot = false
121: 
122: 	n.Cfg = cfg
123: 	for _, o := range opts {
124: 		o(n)
125: 	}
126: 
127: 	nodeType := strings.ToLower(n.Cfg.NodeType)
128: 
129: 	pid := n.Cfg.Index + 1 // n.Cfg.Index is zero-indexed, PID needs to be >= 1
130: 	if off := n.kindSpecificCfg().PidOffset; off != nil {
131: 		pid += *off
132: 	}
133: 
134: 	n.Pid = strconv.Itoa(pid)
135: 
136: 	env := map[string]string{
137: 		"IOL_PID": n.Pid,
138: 	}
139: 
140: 	n.Cfg.Env = clabutils.MergeStringMaps(env, n.Cfg.Env)
141: 
142: 	// check if user submitted node type is valid
143: 	switch nodeType {
144: 	case "":
145: 		n.isL2Node = strings.Contains(strings.ToLower(path.Base(n.Cfg.Image)), "l2")
146: 	case typeIOL:
147: 		n.isL2Node = false
148: 	case typeL2:
149: 		n.isL2Node = true
150: 	default:
151: 		return fmt.Errorf("invalid node type '%s'. Valid types are: %s",
152: 			n.Cfg.NodeType, strings.Join(validTypes, ", "))
153: 	}
154: 
155: 	if err := n.parseMgmtIntf(); err != nil {
156: 		return err
157: 	}
158: 	n.Cfg.Env["CLAB_IOL_MGMT_INTF"] = n.mgmtIntf
159: 
160: 	n.nvramFile = fmt.Sprint("nvram_", fmt.Sprintf("%05s", n.Pid))
161: 
162: 	n.Cfg.Binds = append(n.Cfg.Binds,
163: 		// mount nvram so that config persists
164: 		fmt.Sprint(path.Join(n.Cfg.LabDir, n.nvramFile), ":", path.Join(iol_workdir, n.nvramFile)),
165: 
166: 		// mount launch config
167: 		fmt.Sprint(filepath.Join(n.Cfg.LabDir, "boot_config.txt"), ":/iol/config.txt"),
168: 
169: 		// mount IOYAP and NETMAP for interface mapping
170: 		fmt.Sprint(filepath.Join(n.Cfg.LabDir, "iouyap.ini"), ":/iol/iouyap.ini"),
171: 		fmt.Sprint(filepath.Join(n.Cfg.LabDir, "NETMAP"), ":/iol/NETMAP"),
172: 	)
173: 
174: 	return nil
175: }

177: // parseMgmtIntf parses the mgmt-intf kind-specific config key and normalizes it to
178: // Ethernet<slot>/<port>;
179: // empty means the default Ethernet0/0.
180: func (n *iol) parseMgmtIntf() error {
181: 	if v := n.kindSpecificCfg().MgmtIntf; v != "" {
182: 		captureGroups, err := clabutils.GetRegexpCaptureGroups(CapturingIntfRegexp, v)
183: 		if err != nil {
184: 			return fmt.Errorf("invalid mgmt-intf %q: %w\n%s", v, err, IntfHelpMsg)
185: 		}
186: 
187: 		n.mgmtSlot, _ = strconv.Atoi(captureGroups["slot"])
188: 		n.mgmtPort, _ = strconv.Atoi(captureGroups["port"])
189: 
190: 		if n.mgmtSlot > 9 || n.mgmtPort > 3 {
191: 			return fmt.Errorf(
192: 				"invalid mgmt-intf %q: slot must be 0-9 and port must be 0-3", v)
193: 		}
194: 	}
195: 
196: 	n.mgmtIntf = fmt.Sprintf("Ethernet%d/%d", n.mgmtSlot, n.mgmtPort)
197: 	n.mgmtLinuxIdx = n.mgmtSlot*4 + n.mgmtPort
198: 
199: 	return nil
200: }
201: 
202: // swapMgmtIdx swaps linux interface index 0 with the management interface's index,
203: // so Ethernet0/0 takes the index a relocated management interface vacated.
204: func (n *iol) swapMgmtIdx(idx int) int {
205: 	switch idx {
206: 	case 0:
207: 		return n.mgmtLinuxIdx
208: 	case n.mgmtLinuxIdx:
209: 		return 0
210: 	}
211: 
212: 	return idx
213: }
214: 
215: // iolPortForLinuxIdx returns the IOL slot/port backed by linux interface index idx.
216: func (n *iol) iolPortForLinuxIdx(idx int) (slot, port int) {
217: 	mapped := n.swapMgmtIdx(idx)
218: 	return mapped / 4, mapped % 4
219: }
220: 
221: // ensureNumSlotsEnv exports CLAB_IOL_NUM_SLOTS when the management interface is
222: // relocated, so that its slot exists even when no link lands in it.
223: func (n *iol) ensureNumSlotsEnv() {
224: 	if n.mgmtLinuxIdx == 0 || n.Cfg.Env["CLAB_IOL_NUM_SLOTS"] != "" {
225: 		return
226: 	}
227: 
228: 	intfIdxRegexp := regexp.MustCompile(`\d+`)
229: 	needed := n.mgmtSlot + 1
230: 
231: 	for _, ep := range n.Endpoints {
232: 		x, _ := strconv.Atoi(intfIdxRegexp.FindString(ep.GetIfaceName()))
233: 		if slot, _ := n.iolPortForLinuxIdx(x); slot+1 > needed {
234: 			needed = slot + 1
235: 		}
236: 	}
237: 
238: 	n.Cfg.Env["CLAB_IOL_NUM_SLOTS"] = strconv.Itoa(needed)
239: }
240: 
241: func (n *iol) PreDeploy(ctx context.Context, params *clabnodes.PreDeployParams) error {
242: 	clabutils.CreateDirectory(n.Cfg.LabDir, clabconstants.PermissionsOpen)
243: 
244: 	if v := n.kindSpecificCfg().BootstrapConfig; v != "" {
245: 		switch p := clabutils.ResolvePath(v, params.TopoPaths.TopologyFileDir()); {
246: 		case strings.EqualFold(v, "none"):
247: 			n.bootstrapNone = true
248: 		case clabutils.FileExists(p):
249: 			n.bootstrapCfgFile = p
250: 		default:
251: 			return fmt.Errorf("bootstrap-config file %q does not exist", p)
252: 		}
253: 	}
254: 
255: 	_, err := n.LoadOrGenerateCertificate(params.Cert, params.TopologyName)
256: 	if err != nil {
257: 		return err
258: 	}
259: 
260: 	n.ensureNumSlotsEnv()
261: 
262: 	return n.CreateIOLFiles(ctx)
263: }
264: 
265: func (n *iol) PostDeploy(ctx context.Context, _ *clabnodes.PostDeployParams) error {
266: 	log.Infof("Running postdeploy actions for Cisco IOL '%s' node", n.Cfg.ShortName)
267: 
268: 	n.disableMgmtTxOffload(ctx)
269: 
270: 	if err := n.GenBootConfig(ctx); err != nil {
271: 		return fmt.Errorf("failed to generate boot config: %w", err)
272: 	}
273: 
274: 	// Must update mgmt IP if not first boot, unless the node boots without a baseline config
275: 	if !n.firstBoot && !n.bootstrapNone {
276: 		return n.UpdateMgmtIntf(ctx)
277: 	}
278: 
279: 	return nil
280: }
281: 
282: // Start re-applies the mgmt veth TX offload fix after a stop/start cycle. The runtime
283: // recreates the mgmt veth (it is never parked), so its offload settings reset to defaults.
284: // See disableMgmtTxOffload for why IOL cannot function with it enabled.
285: func (n *iol) Start(ctx context.Context) error {
286: 	if err := n.DefaultNode.Start(ctx); err != nil {
287: 		return err
288: 	}
289: 
290: 	// DefaultNode.Start no-ops for root-ns and auto-remove nodes; skip the offload fix
291: 	// for them as well, as there is no container mgmt veth to adjust.
292: 	if n.ShouldSkipLifecycle() {
293: 		return nil
294: 	}
295: 
296: 	n.disableMgmtTxOffload(ctx)
297: 
298: 	return nil
299: }
300: 
301: // disableMgmtTxOffload disables TX checksum offload on the host NS veth for the mgmt interface.
302: // iouyap bridges IOL interfaces over packet sockets; frames carrying an offloaded
303: // (CHECKSUM_PARTIAL) checksum reach the IOL process with an incomplete checksum, which its
304: // TCP/IP stack drops, breaking TCP/UDP connectivity.
305: //
306: // Nodes running with host network mode are skipped: their eth0 is the host's interface,
307: // and there is no clab-created mgmt veth to adjust.
308: func (n *iol) disableMgmtTxOffload(ctx context.Context) {
309: 	if n.Cfg.NetworkMode == "host" {
310: 		return
311: 	}
312: 
313: 	if n.Runtime.Mgmt().Driver == clabtypes.MgmtDriverMacvlan {
314: 		return
315: 	}
316: 
317: 	var peerIfIndex int
318: 	err := n.ExecFunction(ctx, clabutils.VethPeerIndex("eth0", &peerIfIndex))
319: 	if err != nil {
320: 		log.Warn("Failed to get veth peer index for IOL mgmt interface",
321: 			"node", n.Cfg.ShortName,
322: 			"error", err)
323: 		return
324: 	}
325: 
326: 	if err := clabutils.DisableTxOffloadByIndex(peerIfIndex); err != nil {
327: 		log.Warn("Failed to disable TX checksum offload on IOL mgmt host veth",
328: 			"node", n.Cfg.ShortName,
329: 			"error", err)
330: 	}
331: }
332: 
333: func (n *iol) CreateIOLFiles(ctx context.Context) error {
334: 	// If NVRAM already exists, don't need to create
335: 	// otherwise saved configs in NVRAM are overwritten.
336: 	if !clabutils.FileExists(path.Join(n.Cfg.LabDir, n.nvramFile)) {
337: 		// create nvram file
338: 		clabutils.CreateFile(path.Join(n.Cfg.LabDir, n.nvramFile), "")
339: 		n.firstBoot = true
340: 	}
341: 
342: 	// create these files so the bind monut doesn't automatically
343: 	// make folders.
344: 	clabutils.CreateFile(path.Join(n.Cfg.LabDir, "boot_config.txt"), "")
345: 
346: 	return n.GenInterfaceConfig(ctx)
347: }
348: 
349: // Generate interfaces configuration for IOL (and iouyap/netmap).
350: func (n *iol) GenInterfaceConfig(_ context.Context) error {
351: 	// add 'boilerplate' to NETMAP and iouyap.ini for the management port
352: 	iouyapData := fmt.Sprintf(
353: 		"[default]\nbase_port = 49000\nnetmap = /iol/NETMAP\n[513:%d/%d]\neth_dev = eth0\n",
354: 		n.mgmtSlot,
355: 		n.mgmtPort,
356: 	)
357: 	netmapdata := fmt.Sprintf(
358: 		"%s:%d/%d 513:%d/%d\n",
359: 		n.Pid,
360: 		n.mgmtSlot,
361: 		n.mgmtPort,
362: 		n.mgmtSlot,
363: 		n.mgmtPort,
364: 	)
365: 
366: 	slot, port := 0, 0
367: 
368: 	// Regexp to pull number out of linux'ethX' interface naming
369: 	IntfRegExpr := regexp.MustCompile(`\d+`)
370: 
371: 	for _, intf := range n.Endpoints {
372: 		// get numeric interface number and cast to int
373: 		x, _ := strconv.Atoi(IntfRegExpr.FindString(intf.GetIfaceName()))
374: 
375: 		// Interface naming is Ethernet{slot}/{port}. Each slot contains max 4 ports
376: 		slot, port = n.iolPortForLinuxIdx(x)
377: 
378: 		// append data to write to NETMAP and IOUYAP files
379: 		iouyapData += fmt.Sprintf("[513:%d/%d]\neth_dev = %s\n", slot, port, intf.GetIfaceName())
380: 		netmapdata += fmt.Sprintf("%s:%d/%d 513:%d/%d\n", n.Pid, slot, port, slot, port)
381: 
382: 		// populate template array for config
383: 		ipv4Addr := ""
384: 		ipv4Mask := ""
385: 		v4Prefix := intf.GetIPv4Addr()
386: 		ipv6Addr := ""
387: 
388: 		if v4Prefix.IsValid() {
389: 			ipv4Addr = v4Prefix.Addr().String()
390: 			ipv4Mask = clabutils.CIDRToDDN(v4Prefix.Bits())
391: 		}
392: 
393: 		if a := intf.GetIPv6Addr(); a.IsValid() {
394: 			ipv6Addr = a.String()
395: 		}
396: 
397: 		n.interfaces = append(n.interfaces,
398: 			IOLInterface{
399: 				IfaceName: intf.GetIfaceName(),
400: 				IfaceIdx:  x,
401: 				Slot:      slot,
402: 				Port:      port,
403: 				IPv4Addr:  ipv4Addr,
404: 				IPv4Mask:  ipv4Mask,
405: 				IPv6Addr:  ipv6Addr,
406: 			},
407: 		)
408: 	}
409: 
410: 	// create IOUYAP and NETMAP file for interface mappings
411: 	err := clabutils.CreateFile(path.Join(n.Cfg.LabDir, "iouyap.ini"), iouyapData)
412: 	if err != nil {
413: 		return err
414: 	}
415: 	err = clabutils.CreateFile(path.Join(n.Cfg.LabDir, "NETMAP"), netmapdata)
416: 
417: 	return err
418: }

420: func (n *iol) GenBootConfig(_ context.Context) error {
421: 	n.bootCfg = cfgTemplate
422: 
423: 	switch {
424: 	case n.bootstrapNone:
425: 		n.bootCfg = "{{ .PartialCfg }}"
426: 	case n.bootstrapCfgFile != "":
427: 		cfg, err := os.ReadFile(n.bootstrapCfgFile)
428: 		if err != nil {
429: 			return err
430: 		}
431: 
432: 		n.bootCfg = string(cfg)
433: 	}
434: 
435: 	if n.Cfg.StartupConfig != "" {
436: 		cfg, err := os.ReadFile(n.Cfg.StartupConfig)
437: 		if err != nil {
438: 			return err
439: 		}
440: 
441: 		if clabutils.IsPartialConfigFile(n.Cfg.StartupConfig) {
442: 			n.partialStartupCfg = string(cfg)
443: 		} else {
444: 			n.bootCfg = string(cfg)
445: 		}
446: 	}

448: 	// create startup config template
449: 	tpl := IOLTemplateData{
450: 		Hostname:           n.Cfg.ShortName,
451: 		IsL2Node:           n.isL2Node,
452: 		MgmtIntf:           n.mgmtIntf,
453: 		MgmtIPv4Addr:       n.Cfg.MgmtIPv4Address,
454: 		MgmtIPv4SubnetMask: clabutils.CIDRToDDN(n.Cfg.MgmtIPv4PrefixLength),
455: 		MgmtIPv4GW:         n.Cfg.MgmtIPv4Gateway,
456: 		MgmtIPv6Addr:       n.Cfg.MgmtIPv6Address,
457: 		MgmtIPv6PrefixLen:  n.Cfg.MgmtIPv6PrefixLength,
458: 		MgmtIPv6GW:         n.Cfg.MgmtIPv6Gateway,
459: 		DataIFaces:         n.interfaces,
460: 	}

463: 	if n.partialStartupCfg != "" {

465: 			clabutils.CreateFuncs()).Parse(n.partialStartupCfg)

468: 		}

473: 		}

476: 	}
477: 
478: 	IOLCfgTpl, err := template.New("clab-iol-default-config").Funcs(
479: 		clabutils.CreateFuncs()).Parse(n.bootCfg)
480: 	if err != nil {
481: 		return err
482: 	}
483: 
484: 	// generate the config
485: 	buf := new(bytes.Buffer)
486: 	err = IOLCfgTpl.Execute(buf, tpl)
487: 	if err != nil {
488: 		return err
489: 	}
490: 
491: 	return clabutils.CreateFile(path.Join(n.Cfg.LabDir, "boot_config.txt"), buf.String())
492: }

494: type IOLTemplateData struct {
495: 	Hostname           string
496: 	IsL2Node           bool
497: 	MgmtIntf           string
498: 	MgmtIPv4Addr       string
499: 	MgmtIPv4SubnetMask string

508: // IOLInterface struct stores mapping info between
509: // IOL interface name and linux container interface.
510: type IOLInterface struct {
511: 	IfaceName string
512: 	IfaceIdx  int
513: 	Slot      int
514: 	Port      int
515: 	IPv4Addr  string
516: 	IPv4Mask  string
517: 	IPv6Addr  string
518: }
519: 
520: func (n *iol) GetMappedInterfaceName(ifName string) (string, error) {
521: 	captureGroups, err := clabutils.GetRegexpCaptureGroups(CapturingIntfRegexp, ifName)
522: 	if err != nil {
523: 		return "", err
524: 	}
525: 
526: 	indexGroups := []string{"slot", "port"}
527: 	parsedIndices := make(map[string]int)
528: 	foundIndices := make(map[string]bool)
529: 
530: 	for _, indexKey := range indexGroups {
531: 		if index, found := captureGroups[indexKey]; found && index != "" {
532: 			foundIndices[indexKey] = true
533: 			parsedIndices[indexKey], err = strconv.Atoi(index)
534: 			if err != nil {
535: 				return "", fmt.Errorf(
536: 					"%q parsed %s index %q could not be cast to an integer",
537: 					ifName,
538: 					indexKey,
539: 					index,
540: 				)
541: 			}
542: 			if parsedIndices[indexKey] < 0 {
543: 				return "", fmt.Errorf(
544: 					"%q parsed %q index %q does not match requirement >= 0",
545: 					ifName,
546: 					indexKey,
547: 					index,
548: 				)
549: 			}
550: 		} else {
551: 			foundIndices[indexKey] = false
552: 		}
553: 	}
554: 
555: 	// return an ethX interface name. Slots are in 'groups' of 4 interfaces each
556: 	if foundIndices["slot"] && foundIndices["port"] {
557: 		idx := n.swapMgmtIdx((parsedIndices["slot"] * 4) + parsedIndices["port"])
558: 		return fmt.Sprintf("eth%d", idx), nil
559: 	} else {
560: 		return "", fmt.Errorf("%q missing slot or port index", ifName)
561: 	}
562: }
563: 
564: // AddEndpoint override maps the endpoint name to an ethX-based naming where necessary, before
565: // adding it to the node endpoints. Returns an error if the mapping goes wrong or if the
566: // interface name is NOT allowed.
567: func (n *iol) AddEndpoint(e clablinks.Endpoint) error {
568: 	endpointName := e.GetIfaceName()
569: 	var IFaceName, IFaceAlias string
570: 
571: 	IFaceName = endpointName
572: 
573: 	if !(DefaultIntfRegexp.MatchString(endpointName)) &&
574: 		AllowedIntfRegexp.MatchString(endpointName) {
575: 		log.Debugf("%s: %s needs mapping", n.Cfg.ShortName, endpointName)
576: 		mappedName, err := n.GetMappedInterfaceName(endpointName)
577: 		if err != nil {
578: 			return fmt.Errorf(
579: 				"%q interface name %q could not be mapped to an ethX-based interface name: %w\n%s",
580: 				n.Cfg.ShortName,
581: 				e.GetIfaceName(),
582: 				err,
583: 				IntfHelpMsg,
584: 			)
585: 		}
586: 		log.Debugf(
587: 			"Interface Mapping: Mapping interface %q (ifAlias) to %q (ifName)",
588: 			endpointName,
589: 			mappedName,
590: 		)
591: 		IFaceName = mappedName
592: 		IFaceAlias = endpointName
593: 	}
594: 
595: 	e.SetIfaceName(IFaceName)
596: 	// should be nil if ethX naming is used.
597: 	e.SetIfaceAlias(IFaceAlias)
598: 	n.Endpoints = append(n.Endpoints, e)
599: 
600: 	return nil
601: }
602: 
603: func (n *iol) CheckInterfaceName() error {
604: 	err := n.CheckInterfaceOverlap()
605: 	if err != nil {
606: 		return err
607: 	}
608: 
609: 	for _, e := range n.Endpoints {
610: 		IFaceName := e.GetIfaceName()
611: 		// eth0 always backs the management interface
612: 		if IFaceName == "eth0" {
613: 			return fmt.Errorf(
614: 				"IOL Node: %q. Management interface %s (eth0) is not allowed",
615: 				n.Cfg.ShortName,
616: 				n.mgmtIntf,
617: 			)
618: 		}
619: 
620: 		if !DefaultIntfRegexp.MatchString(IFaceName) {
621: 			return fmt.Errorf(
622: 				"IOL Node %q has an interface named %q which doesn't match the required pattern. %s",
623: 				n.Cfg.ShortName,
624: 				IFaceName,
625: 				IntfHelpMsg,
626: 			)
627: 		}
628: 	}
629: 
630: 	return nil
631: }
632: 
633: func (n *iol) UpdateMgmtIntf(ctx context.Context) error {
634: 	// L2 IOL images default to switchport mode, which rejects IP addresses.
635: 	switchportCmd := ""
636: 	if n.isL2Node {
637: 		switchportCmd = "no switchport\r"
638: 	}
639: 
640: 	waitCtx, cancel := context.WithTimeout(ctx, bootPromptTimeout)
641: 	defer cancel()
642: 
643: 	// 1. Start streaming container logs
644: 	logReader, err := n.Runtime.StreamLogs(waitCtx, n.Cfg.LongName)
645: 	if err != nil {
646: 		log.Warn("Skipping IOL mgmt interface update, cannot stream container logs",
647: 			"node", n.Cfg.ShortName, "error", err)
648: 		return nil
649: 	}
650: 	defer logReader.Close()
651: 
652: 	// Ticker to periodically send carriage returns (\r) to wake up the serial console
653: 	ticker := time.NewTicker(2 * time.Second)
654: 	defer ticker.Stop()
655: 
656: 	// Scanner to read the container log output line by line
657: 	scanner := bufio.NewScanner(logReader)
658: 
659: 	// Asynchronously send carriage returns since scanner.Scan() blocks
660: 	tickCtx, stopTicker := context.WithCancel(waitCtx)
661: 	defer stopTicker()
662: 	tickerDone := make(chan struct{})
663: 
664: 	go func() {
665: 		defer close(tickerDone)
666: 		for {
667: 			select {
668: 			case <-tickCtx.Done():
669: 				return
670: 			case <-ticker.C:
671: 				_ = n.Runtime.WriteToStdinNoWait(tickCtx, n.Cfg.LongName, []byte("\r"))
672: 			}
673: 		}
674: 	}()
675: 
676: 	bootComplete := false
677: 	for scanner.Scan() {
678: 		// Trim control characters and spaces from the log line
679: 		line := strings.TrimSpace(scanner.Text())
680: 		if line == "" {
681: 			continue
682: 		}
683: 
684: 		log.Debugf("[IOL Boot Log] %s", line)
685: 
686: 		// Match boot prompts (>, #, or initial config dialog)
687: 		if strings.HasSuffix(line, ">") ||
688: 			strings.HasSuffix(line, "#") ||
689: 			strings.Contains(line, "initial configuration dialog?") {
690: 
691: 			// Ignore syslog messages containing '>'
692: 			if strings.Contains(line, "%") {
693: 				continue
694: 			}
695: 
696: 			log.Infof("IOL boot prompt detected (%s). Proceeding with configuration.", line)
697: 			bootComplete = true
698: 			break
699: 		}
700: 	}
701: 
702: 	stopTicker()
703: 	<-tickerDone
704: 
705: 	if !bootComplete {
706: 		if ctx.Err() != nil {
707: 			return ctx.Err()
708: 		}
709: 		if waitCtx.Err() != nil {
710: 			return fmt.Errorf("node %q: timed out after %s waiting for IOL boot prompt",
711: 				n.Cfg.ShortName, bootPromptTimeout)
712: 		}
713: 		if err := scanner.Err(); err != nil {
714: 			return fmt.Errorf("error reading IOL log stream: %w", err)
715: 		}
716: 	}
717: 	// --- End of prompt detection ---
718: 
719: 	// All IOS commands applied here are idempotent and safe to re-run.
720: 	mgmt_str := fmt.Sprintf(
721: 		"\rend\renable\rconfig terminal\rinterface %s\r%sip address %s %s\rno ipv6 address\ripv6 address %s/%d\rexit\rip route vrf clab-mgmt 0.0.0.0 0.0.0.0 %s %s\ripv6 route vrf clab-mgmt ::/0 %s %s\rend\rwr\r",
722: 		n.mgmtIntf,
723: 		switchportCmd,
724: 		n.Cfg.MgmtIPv4Address,
725: 		clabutils.CIDRToDDN(n.Cfg.MgmtIPv4PrefixLength),
726: 		n.Cfg.MgmtIPv6Address,
727: 		n.Cfg.MgmtIPv6PrefixLength,
728: 		n.mgmtIntf,
729: 		n.Cfg.MgmtIPv4Gateway,
730: 		n.mgmtIntf,
731: 		n.Cfg.MgmtIPv6Gateway,
732: 	)
733: 	data := []byte(mgmt_str)
734: 
735: 	var lastErr error
736: 	for i := range 3 {
737: 		if i > 0 {
738: 			time.Sleep(10 * time.Second)
739: 		}
740: 		lastErr = n.Runtime.WriteToStdinNoWait(ctx, n.Cfg.ContainerID, data)
741: 		if lastErr == nil {
742: 			break
743: 		}
744: 		log.Warnf("UpdateMgmtIntf: attempt %d/3 failed for %s: %v",
745: 			i+1, n.Cfg.ShortName, lastErr)
746: 	}
747: 	return lastErr
748: }
749: 
750: // SaveConfig is used for "clab save" functionality -- it saves the running config to the startup
751: // configuration.
752: func (n *iol) SaveConfig(_ context.Context) (*clabnodes.SaveConfigResult, error) {
753: 	p, err := platform.NewPlatform(
754: 		"cisco_iosxe",
755: 		n.Cfg.LongName,
756: 		options.WithAuthNoStrictKey(),
757: 		options.WithAuthUsername(n.Cfg.Credentials.Username),
758: 		options.WithAuthPassword(n.Cfg.Credentials.Password),
759: 	)
760: 	if err != nil {
761: 		return nil, fmt.Errorf("failed to create platform; error: %+v", err)
762: 	}
763: 
764: 	d, err := p.GetNetworkDriver()
765: 	if err != nil {
766: 		return nil, fmt.Errorf("failed to fetch network driver from the platform; error: %+v", err)
767: 	}
768: 
769: 	err = d.Open()
770: 	if err != nil {
771: 		return nil, fmt.Errorf("failed to open driver; error: %+v", err)
772: 	}
773: 
774: 	defer d.Close()
775: 
776: 	_, err = d.SendCommand("write memory")
777: 	if err != nil {
778: 		return nil, fmt.Errorf("failed to send command; error: %+v", err)
779: 	}
780: 
781: 	log.Infof(
782: 		"Successfully copied running configuration to startup configuration file for node: %q\n",
783: 		n.Cfg.ShortName,
784: 	)
785: 	return nil, nil
786: }

v != \"\" {\n\t\tcaptureGroups, err := clabutils.GetRegexpCaptureGroups(CapturingIntfRegexp, v)\n\t\tif err != nil {\n\t\t\treturn fmt.Errorf(\"invalid mgmt-intf %q: %w\\n%s\", v, err, IntfHelpMsg)\n\t\t}\n\n\t\tn.mgmtSlot, _ = strconv.Atoi(captureGroups[\"slot\"])\n\t\tn.mgmtPort, _ = strconv.Atoi(captureGroups[\"port\"])\n\n\t\tif n.mgmtSlot > 9 || n.mgmtPort > 3 {\n\t\t\treturn fmt.Errorf(\n\t\t\t\t\"invalid mgmt-intf %q: slot must be 0-9 and port must be 0-3\", v)\n\t\t}\n\t}\n\n\tn.mgmtIntf = fmt.Sprintf(\"Ethernet%d/%d\", n.mgmtSlot, n.mgmtPort)\n\tn.mgmtLinuxIdx = n.mgmtSlot*4 + n.mgmtPort\n\n\treturn nil\n}\n\n// swapMgmtIdx swaps linux interface index 0 with the management interface's index,\n// so Ethernet0/0 takes the index a relocated management interface vacated.\nfunc (n *iol) swapMgmtIdx(idx int) int {\n\tswitch idx {\n\tcase 0:\n\t\treturn n.mgmtLinuxIdx\n\tcase n.mgmtLinuxIdx:\n\t\treturn 0\n\t}\n\n\treturn idx\n}\n\n// iolPortForLinuxIdx returns the IOL slot/port backed by linux interface index idx.\nfunc (n *iol) iolPortForLinuxIdx(idx int) (slot, port int) {\n\tmapped := n.swapMgmtIdx(idx)\n\treturn mapped / 4, mapped % 4\n}\n\n// ensureNumSlotsEnv exports CLAB_IOL_NUM_SLOTS when the management interface is\n// relocated, so that its slot exists even when no link lands in it.\nfunc (n *iol) ensureNumSlotsEnv() {\n\tif n.mgmtLinuxIdx == 0 || n.Cfg.Env[\"CLAB_IOL_NUM_SLOTS\"] != \"\" {\n\t\treturn\n\t}\n\n\tintfIdxRegexp := regexp.MustCompile(`\\d+`)\n\tneeded := n.mgmtSlot + 1\n\n\tfor _, ep := range n.Endpoints {\n\t\tx, _ := strconv.Atoi(intfIdxRegexp.FindString(ep.GetIfaceName()))\n\t\tif slot, _ := n.iolPortForLinuxIdx(x); slot+1 > needed {\n\t\t\tneeded = slot + 1\n\t\t}\n\t}\n\n\tn.Cfg.Env[\"CLAB_IOL_NUM_SLOTS\"] = strconv.Itoa(needed)\n}\n\nfunc (n *iol) PreDeploy(ctx context.Context, params *clabnodes.PreDeployParams) error {\n\tclabutils.CreateDirectory(n.Cfg.LabDir, clabconstants.PermissionsOpen)\n\n\tif v := n.kindSpecificCfg().BootstrapConfig; v != \"\" {\n\t\tswitch p := clabutils.ResolvePath(v, params.TopoPaths.TopologyFileDir()); {\n\t\tcase strings.EqualFold(v, \"none\"):\n\t\t\tn.bootstrapNone = true\n\t\tcase clabutils.FileExists(p):\n\t\t\tn.bootstrapCfgFile = p\n\t\tdefault:\n\t\t\treturn fmt.Errorf(\"bootstrap-config file %q does not exist\", p)\n\t\t}\n\t}\n\n\t_, err := n.LoadOrGenerateCertificate(params.Cert, params.TopologyName)\n\tif err != nil {\n\t\treturn err\n\t}\n\n\tn.ensureNumSlotsEnv()\n\n\treturn n.CreateIOLFiles(ctx)\n}\n\nfunc (n *iol) PostDeploy(ctx context.Context, _ *clabnodes.PostDeployParams) error {\n\tlog.Infof(\"Running postdeploy actions for Cisco IOL '%s' node\", n.Cfg.ShortName)\n\n\tn.disableMgmtTxOffload(ctx)\n\n\tif err := n.GenBootConfig(ctx); err != nil {\n\t\treturn fmt.Errorf(\"failed to generate boot config: %w\", err)\n\t}\n\n\t// Must update mgmt IP if not first boot, unless the node boots without a baseline config\n\tif !n.firstBoot && !n.bootstrapNone {\n\t\treturn n.UpdateMgmtIntf(ctx)\n\t}\n\n\treturn nil\n}\n\n// Start re-applies the mgmt veth TX offload fix after a stop/start cycle. The runtime\n// recreates the mgmt veth (it is never parked), so its offload settings reset to defaults.\n// See disableMgmtTxOffload for why IOL cannot function with it enabled.\nfunc (n *iol) Start(ctx context.Context) error {\n\tif err := n.DefaultNode.Start(ctx); err != nil {\n\t\treturn err\n\t}\n\n\t// DefaultNode.Start no-ops for root-ns and auto-remove nodes; skip the offload fix\n\t// for them as well, as there is no container mgmt veth to adjust.\n\tif n.ShouldSkipLifecycle() {\n\t\treturn nil\n\t}\n\n\tn.disableMgmtTxOffload(ctx)\n\n\treturn nil\n}\n\n// disableMgmtTxOffload disables TX checksum offload on the host NS veth for the mgmt interface.\n// iouyap bridges IOL interfaces over packet sockets; frames carrying an offloaded\n// (CHECKSUM_PARTIAL) checksum reach the IOL process with an incomplete checksum, which its\n// TCP/IP stack drops, breaking TCP/UDP connectivity.\n//\n// Nodes running with host network mode are skipped: their eth0 is the host's interface,\n// and there is no clab-created mgmt veth to adjust.\nfunc (n *iol) disableMgmtTxOffload(ctx context.Context) {\n\tif n.Cfg.NetworkMode == \"host\" {\n\t\treturn\n\t}\n\n\tif n.Runtime.Mgmt().Driver == clabtypes.MgmtDriverMacvlan {\n\t\treturn\n\t}\n\n\tvar peerIfIndex int\n\terr := n.ExecFunction(ctx, clabutils.VethPeerIndex(\"eth0\", &peerIfIndex))\n\tif err != nil {\n\t\tlog.Warn(\"Failed to get veth peer index for IOL mgmt interface\",\n\t\t\t\"node\", n.Cfg.ShortName,\n\t\t\t\"error\", err)\n\t\treturn\n\t}\n\n\tif err := clabutils.DisableTxOffloadByIndex(peerIfIndex); err != nil {\n\t\tlog.Warn(\"Failed to disable TX checksum offload on IOL mgmt host veth\",\n\t\t\t\"node\", n.Cfg.ShortName,\n\t\t\t\"error\", err)\n\t}\n}\n\nfunc (n *iol) CreateIOLFiles(ctx context.Context) error {\n\t// If NVRAM already exists, don't need to create\n\t// otherwise saved configs in NVRAM are overwritten.\n\tif !clabutils.FileExists(path.Join(n.Cfg.LabDir, n.nvramFile)) {\n\t\t// create nvram file\n\t\tclabutils.CreateFile(path.Join(n.Cfg.LabDir, n.nvramFile), \"\")\n\t\tn.firstBoot = true\n\t}\n\n\t// create these files so the bind monut doesn't automatically\n\t// make folders.\n\tclabutils.CreateFile(path.Join(n.Cfg.LabDir, \"boot_config.txt\"), \"\")\n\n\treturn n.GenInterfaceConfig(ctx)\n}\n\n// Generate interfaces configuration for IOL (and iouyap/netmap).\nfunc (n *iol) GenInterfaceConfig(_ context.Context) error {\n\t// add 'boilerplate' to NETMAP and iouyap.ini for the management port\n\tiouyapData := fmt.Sprintf(\n\t\t\"[default]\\nbase_port = 49000\\nnetmap = /iol/NETMAP\\n[513:%d/%d]\\neth_dev = eth0\\n\",\n\t\tn.mgmtSlot,\n\t\tn.mgmtPort,\n\t)\n\tnetmapdata := fmt.Sprintf(\n\t\t\"%s:%d/%d 513:%d/%d\\n\",\n\t\tn.Pid,\n\t\tn.mgmtSlot,\n\t\tn.mgmtPort,\n\t\tn.mgmtSlot,\n\t\tn.mgmtPort,\n\t)\n\n\tslot, port := 0, 0\n\n\t// Regexp to pull number out of linux'ethX' interface naming\n\tIntfRegExpr := regexp.MustCompile(`\\d+`)\n\n\tfor _, intf := range n.Endpoints {\n\t\t// get numeric interface number and cast to int\n\t\tx, _ := strconv.Atoi(IntfRegExpr.FindString(intf.GetIfaceName()))\n\n\t\t// Interface naming is Ethernet{slot}/{port}. step-start
{
  "type": "step-start",
  "id": "prt_1f9a64b83001MiT3MUvrIYp6RU",
  "sessionID": "ses_e0659f136ffeQ7NPh5YPoIIBpb",
  "messageID": "msg_1f9a64357001xWJ0GAQgsYp2Rc"
}
Reasoning
The PR adds one line: `ipv6 nd ra suppress all` on the management interface in the IOL config template.

Now let me think about whether this is correct and complete. The mgmt interface uses `vrf forwarding clab-mgmt` with an ipv6 address. The rationale for `ipv6 nd ra suppress all` is likely to stop the node from sending Router Advertisements on the management network, which could interfere with other hosts on the mgmt network (e.g., host machines doing SLAAC). That's a reasonable, common IOS change.

Key considerations:

1. Is `ipv6 nd ra suppress all` valid IOS syntax on an interface? Yes, `ipv6 nd ra suppress all` suppresses RA transmission and also disables responding to RS. Valid on IOS interfaces (with ipv6 unicast-routing globally enabled, which it is, line 9).

2. Does `UpdateMgmtIntf` need the same change? The postdeploy path re-applies mgmt config for existing nodes (not first boot). Looking at `mgmt_str`, it reapplies: `no ipv6 address`, `ipv6 address ...`. It does NOT reapply `ipv6 nd ra suppress all`. But wait — the boot config is generated fresh each deploy into boot_config.txt, but the node only reads boot config on first boot (nvram persists). For non-first boots, the running config persists in nvram, so the RA suppress config would already be in the saved config from first boot... unless the user saved config. Hmm, but if the user's saved config exists from before this change (existing labs), the new line won't be applied and `UpdateMgmtIntf` doesn't add it. So for existing labs created with the old template, the RA suppress command will never be applied. But is that a defect? For labs where nvram was already saved with old config, the new line wouldn't apply. The UpdateMgmtIntf reapplies idempotent mgmt commands; it explicitly says "All IOS commands applied here are idempotent and safe to re-run." Adding `ipv6 nd ra suppress all` there would make existing labs converge. That's a plausible P3/P4 finding — consistency between the bootstrap template and the postdeploy mgmt update path. Is it a correctness defect? On first boot after this change, the template includes the command, so new labs are fine. For an existing lab whose nvram contains old config, redeploy won't add the suppress command. The impact: RAs continue to be emitted on the mgmt network for pre-existing labs — the very problem the PR tries to fix. That's a genuine finding worth noting — P3 perhaps.

Wait — actually, does the mgmt RA suppression matter? Why suppress RAs? On IOL, the mgmt interface is in VRF clab-mgmt with an IPv6 address. If RAs are sent onto the management network (the containerlab mgmt bridge/docker network), other containers on that bridge that do SLAAC could pick up routes. Actually more important: the IOL node itself may respond to Router Solicitations or send RAs claiming to be a router on the mgmt network, causing other nodes to install it as a default router, breaking their mgmt connectivity. Also, the IOL node itself with `ipv6 unicast-routing` will send RAs. Suppression is a sane default.

Also consider: does suppression affect the IOL node's own ability to use its mgmt IPv6? No — suppressing RAs doesn't stop the interface from having an address or routing. It stops advertising. So the fix is fine for first boots.

Another consideration: does the `UpdateMgmtIntf` string do `no ipv6 address` then re-add? It could be that when mgmt IPv6 is empty, `ipv6 address /0` would be invalid... not part of this PR though.

3. Docs: Is there documentation for cisco_iol kinds mentioning the bootstrap config? The change adds a line to the default bootstrap config; docs for cisco_iol (docs/manual/kinds/cisco_iol.md) may show the boot config or describe bootstrap-config option. Probably no doc change needed for a one-line config addition. But maybe the docs show example boot config. Let me check.

4. Tests: The test file has tests for UpdateMgmtIntf and L2 detection; no template test. A one-line template change doesn't need a test really. But could add a check that rendered config includes the command. Per ponytail: "non-trivial logic leaves ONE runnable check behind" — this is a one-liner, trivial, no test needed.

5. Schema: no topology schema change needed.

Let me check whether similar suppression exists in other node templates for consistency — e.g., other Cisco nodes. Let me grep for "nd ra suppress" in the repo.

Also check the git-mutations rule file... not needed.

Let me grep for the command in the repo and check docs for cisco_iol to see if the bootstrap config is documented.
The PR is a single-line change to the Cisco IOL bootstrap config template. Let me check the related code paths and docs for consistency.

This is useful to preconfigure certain things like loopback interfaces or IGP, while also taking advantage of the startup configuration that containerlab applies by default for management interface IP addressing and SSH access.\n\n  Line 190: The partial startup configuration must contain `.partial` in the filename. Templating also supported with partial configs.\n\n  Line 198:       startup-config: configuration.txt.partial\n\n  Line 201: #### Custom baseline configuration\n\n  Line 203: The default startup configuration that containerlab applies (management addressing, management VRF, SSH) can be replaced or disabled with the `bootstrap-config` [kind-specific config key](../nodes.md#kind-specific-config), without giving up partial startup configurations. This is useful you need to manage advanced configuration sets for labs or set up custom management:\n\n  Line 209:       bootstrap-config: my-baseline.cfg # replaces the default startup configuration\n\n  Line 212:       startup-config: r1.partial.cfg # layered on top of my-baseline.cfg\n\n  Line 215: The file replaces the [default startup configuration template](https://github.com/srl-labs/containerlab/blob/main/nodes/iol/iol.cfg.tmpl) and is rendered with the same template variables; include `{{ .PartialCfg }}` where partial startup configurations should be inserted. Relative paths are resolved against the topology file directory, the same as `startup-config`.\n\n  Line 217: Setting `bootstrap-config: none` disables the baseline entirely: the node boots with only its partial startup configuration, or with no configuration at all. In that case containerlab also skips the management interface address update on subsequent boots.\n\n  Line 221: Cisco IOL kind supports [IPv4/IPv6 link addresses](../topo-def-file.md#ip-addresses) that are used to configure IP addresses on the point to point links between the nodes by usage of `ipv4` and/or `ipv6` fields in the link definition. Refer to the linked section for more information and examples.\n\n  Line 223: The result of defining IPv4/IPv6 addresses via the respective fields in the links section is that the IPv4 and/or IPv6 addresses will be configured under the `interface` section of the configuration block.\n\n  Line 227: IOL-L2 requires a different startup configuration compared to the regular IOL. <content>
80: - `eth1` - First data-plane interface. Mapped to `Ethernet0/1` interface.
81: - `eth2` - Second data-plane interface. Mapped to `Ethernet0/2` interface
82: - `eth3` - Third data-plane interface. Mapped to `Ethernet0/3` interface
83: - `eth4` - Fourth data-plane interface. Mapped to `Ethernet1/0` interface
84: - `eth5` - Fifth data-plane interface. Mapped to `Ethernet1/1` interface and so on...
85: 
86: When containerlab launches -{{ kind_display_name }}-, the `Ethernet0/0` interface of the container gets assigned management IPv4 and IPv6 addresses from docker. The `Ethernet0/0` interface is in it's own management VRF so that configuration in the global context will not affect the management interface.
87: 
88: Interfaces can be defined in a non-contiguous manner in your topology file. See the example below.
89: 
90: ```yaml
91: name: my-iol-lab
92: topology:
93:   nodes:
94:     iol-1:
95:       kind: cisco_iol
96:       image: vrnetlab/cisco_iol:17.12.01
97:     iol-2:
98:       kind: cisco_iol
99:       image: vrnetlab/cisco_iol:17.12.01
100: 
101:   links:
102:     - endpoints: ["iol-1:Ethernet0/1","iol-2:Ethernet0/1"] 
103:     - endpoints: ["iol-1:Ethernet1/3", "iol-2:Ethernet1/0"]
104: ```
105: 
106: /// warning
107: When defining interfaces non-contigiously you may see more interfaces than you have defined in the -{{ kind_short_display_name }}- CLI, this is because interfaces are provisioned in groups.
108: 
109: At minimum you will see all numerically-lower indexed interfaces in the CLI compared to the interface you have defined, you may also see interfaces with a higher numerical index.
110: 
111: **Links/interfaces that you did not define in your containerlab topology will *not* pass any traffic.**
112: ///
113: 
114: ### Management interface
115: 
116: By default the containerlab reserves an interface on the router for containerlab management access.
117: 
118: By default and for simplicity, this interface is set to `Ethernet0/0`.
119: 
120: If you intend to use `Ethernet0/0` for links in your topology, the management interface can be changed with the `mgmt-intf` [kind-specific config key](../nodes.md#kind-specific-config).
121: 
122: ```yaml
123: topology:
124:   nodes:
125:     iol1:
126:       kind: cisco_iol
127:       image: ...
128:       mgmt-intf: Ethernet3/3
129: ```
130: 
131: With the example above the management VRF, addressing and default routes move to `Ethernet3/3`. Furthermore links on `Ethernet3/3` are rejected instead of `Ethernet0/0`, and `Ethernet0/0` becomes available to use as a data interface in the `links` section of the topology.
132: 
133: /// note
134: Automatic slot allocation requires an IOL image built with [srl-labs/vrnetlab PR #526](https://github.com/srl-labs/vrnetlab/pull/526) or later.
135: 
136: Images built from an older vrnetlab revision are not compatible.
137: ///
138: 
139: Data interfaces other than the selected management interface need to be configured with IP addressing manually using CLI or other available management interfaces and will appear `unset` in the CLI. With the default management interface:
140: 
141: ```
142: iol#sh ip int br
143: Interface              IP-Address      OK? Method Status                Protocol
144: Ethernet0/0            172.20.20.5     YES TFTP   up                    up
145: Ethernet0/1            unassigned      YES unset  administratively down down
146: Ethernet0/2            unassigned      YES unset  administratively down down
147: Ethernet0/3            unassigned      YES unset  administratively down down
148: ```
149: 
150: ## Startup configuration
151: 
152: When -{{ kind_display_name }}- is booted, it will start with a basic configuration which configures the following:
153: 
154: - IP addressing for the Ethernet0/0 (management) interface.
155: - Management VRF for the Ethernet0/0 interface.
156: - Default route(s) in the management VRF context for the [management network](../network.md#management-network).
157: - SSH server.
158: - Sets all user defined interfaces into 'up' state.
159: 
160: On subsequent boots (deployments which are not the first boot of -{{ kind_short_display_name }}-), -{{ kind_short_display_name }}- will take a few extra seconds to come up, this is because Containerlab must update the management interface IP addressing and default routes for the management network.
161: 
162: ### User-defined config
163: 
164: -{{ kind_display_name }}- supports user defined startup configurations in two forms:
165: 
166: - Full startup configuration.
167: - Partial startup configuration.
168: 
169: Both types of startup configurations are only be applied on the **first boot** of -{{ kind_short_display_name }}-. When you save configuration in IOL to the NVRAM (using `write memory` or `copy run start` commands), the NVRAM configuration will override the startup configuration.

The docs enumerate what the basic config configures; RA suppression is not enumerated, but the list isn't exhaustive of every line (it mentions "IP addressing", "VRF", "default routes", "SSH", interfaces up). Not strictly required to update. But a release note may be relevant — the .cursor/rules/release-note-review.mdc exists. Let me read it. Also check whether there's a release note pattern requirement (docs/rn/0.83.md is latest). Let me read the release-note-review rule.

<content>
1: ---
2: description: Verify release-note mutability when reviewing PR documentation
3: alwaysApply: true
4: ---
5: 
6: # Release-note review
7: 
8: When reviewing a PR, inspect any changes under `docs/rn/`:
9: 
10: - Verify the target release is still unreleased before treating the change as valid.
11: - Treat release notes for published releases as immutable; flag edits or additions there.
12: - Recommend moving new content to the next unreleased release note or the appropriate evergreen documentation.

(End of file - total 12 lines)
</content>

1: ---
2: description: Require explicit user authorization for Git mutations
3: alwaysApply: true
4: ---
5: 
6: # Git mutation policy
7: 
8: - Never automatically commit, amend, push, force-push, or create/update a pull request.
9: - Leave ordinary fixes as working-tree changes unless the user explicitly requests the specific Git or GitHub action.
10: - A request to implement or fix code alone does not authorize a commit or push.

(End of file - total 10 lines)
</content>

Output

<path>/source/head/docs/rn/0.83.md</path>
<type>file</type>
<content>
1: # Release 0.83
2: 
3: :material-calendar: 2026-11-06 · :material-list-status: [Full Changelog](https://github.com/srl-labs/containerlab/releases)
4: 
5: ## ALE OmniSwitch vAOS
6: 
7: The new [`ale_vaos`](../manual/kinds/ale_vaos.md) kind adds support for Alcatel-Lucent Enterprise OmniSwitch virtual AOS 8.x as a vrnetlab-backed QEMU VM:
8: 
9: ```yaml
10: topology:
11:   nodes:
12:     os1:
13:       kind: ale_vaos
14:       image: vrnetlab/ale_vaos:8.11.162.R01
15:       startup-config: os1.cfg
16: ```
17: 
18: Data interfaces `eth1` through `eth10` map to vAOS ports `1/1/1` through `1/1/10`, with `eth11` and `eth12` mapping to `1/1/29A` and `1/1/30A`. The launcher applies startup configuration over the serial console, configures the EMP management interface and saves the configuration. `containerlab save` writes the output of `show configuration snapshot` back to the node's startup-config file.
19: 
20: See the [ALE OmniSwitch vAOS kind documentation](../manual/kinds/ale_vaos.md) for image acquisition, management access and configuration options.
21: 
22: Done in #3556 thanks to @Mathias-gt
23: 
24: ## Packet capture with `containerlab capture`
25: 
26: The new [`capture`](../cmd/capture.md) command records packets from one or more lab link interfaces into a single pcapng file, or streams them live into Wireshark. It works with Docker, Podman and c9s, without installing capture tools in the nodes:
27: 
28: ```bash
29: # watch both ends of a link live in Wireshark
30: containerlab capture --name srl \
31:   --target srl1:e1-1 --target srl2:e1-1 -w - | wireshark -k -i -
32: 
33: # capture two interfaces for 30 seconds
34: containerlab capture -t srl.clab.yml \
35:   --target srl1:e1-1,e1-2 --duration 30s -w srl.pcapng
36: 
37: # list capturable interfaces and their peers
38: containerlab capture --name srl --list
39: ```
40: 
41: Interfaces can be selected by their name or topology alias, and every packet is associated with its `<node>:<interface>` name in the capture. Packets from all targets are merged in timestamp order. Captures can be bounded by duration or packet count, truncated to a snap length, and stopped cleanly with Ctrl+C while leaving a valid pcapng stream.
42: 
43: With Docker and Podman, containerlab captures through packet sockets in the node network namespaces; the command needs root, like `deploy`, but neither the host nor the node needs `tcpdump`. VLAN tags are preserved, interfaces that go down and back up remain captured, and a node restart is reattached with the missed interval reported as a gap. With c9s, the `clabwire` sidecar captures the packets, and long captures are chained without duplicating packets at the handover.
44: 
45: The [`inspect interfaces`](../cmd/inspect/interfaces.md) command now supports c9s as well, reporting lab interfaces with the same names, aliases and fields as the local runtimes.
46: 
47: Done in #3542 thanks to @FloSch62
48: 
49: ## c9s runtime improvements
50: 
51: /// warning | clabernetes 0.11 or newer required
52: The c9s runtime now requires clabernetes 0.11 or newer, which provides Node run state and the clabwire packet-capture contract. Containerlab checks the cluster's published Node schema before the first cluster operation and reports a clear error for older releases. Offline commands such as `validate`, `deploy --emit-crs` and file-based `graph` do not need cluster access.
53: ///
54: 
55: ### Controller-managed lifecycle and persistent state
56: 
57: `stop`, `start` and `restart` now ask the clabernetes controllers to manage a node through its `Node` resource instead of editing Deployments. A stopped node stays stopped across deploy/apply operations and starts with its updated definition when explicitly started. Nodes sharing a pod through `network-mode: container:` follow their primary node's lifecycle.
58: 
59: Persistent node volumes now survive a plain `destroy`, just as a local lab directory does. A subsequent deploy adopts the retained PersistentVolumeClaims and boots from the saved device state. `destroy --cleanup` removes the claims and managed namespace, while `deploy --reconfigure` and `redeploy --cleanup` also start from clean storage.
60: 
61: ### Docker-compatible inspection and events
62: 
63: The c9s runtime now presents nodes using Docker's state and event vocabulary, allowing clients to consume local and cluster labs in the same way:
64: 
65: - `inspect` reports `created`, `running` and `exited` states, stable `<namespace>/<lab>/<node>` container IDs, the deployed topology file, and c9s-specific pod, container, Node UID, namespace, exposed-port and LoadBalancer fields in JSON output.
66: - Container events use familiar actions such as `create`, `start`, `stop`, `die`, `destroy` and `health_status`, with the corresponding containerlab labels and node attributes.
67: - Interface events and `inspect interfaces` expose the node's lab links, management interface and loopback under their Docker-equivalent names, while hiding clabernetes' internal pod interfaces.
68: - Events can be scoped to one lab or namespace, and `inspect --all` and `destroy --all` honor the namespace override.
69: - `graph --mermaid`, `--dot`, `--drawio` and HTML with `--offline` render directly from a topology file without connecting to a cluster.
70: 
71: Done in #3539, #3541 and #3543, thanks to @FloSch62
72: 
73: ## Node logs and lab-runtime access
74: 
75: The new [`logs`](../cmd/logs.md) command displays or follows a node's log with Docker, Podman and c9s. With c9s it reads the log of the node's primary device container:
76: 
77: ```bash
78: containerlab logs --name clos -n srl1 --tail 100 --follow --timestamps
79: ```
80: 
81: Containerlab's Go API gained lab-runtime interfaces for locating nodes, opening interactive terminals, streaming logs, forwarding TCP ports, sampling CPU and memory usage, and capturing packets. The c9s implementation supports Kubernetes user impersonation per runtime or per call, applying the selected identity to REST requests, watches, logs, exec and port-forward connections. These APIs let services such as clab-api-server provide node access while Kubernetes RBAC continues to authorize each user.
82: 
83: Done in #3540 thanks to @FloSch62
84: 
85: ## Selecting the management network of `mgmt-net` links
86: 
87: With [multiple management networks](../manual/network.md#multiple-management-networks), an extended [`mgmt-net` link](../manual/network.md#additional-connections-to-management-network) can now select its attachment network with the `network` key:
88: 
89: ```yaml
90: name: multi
91: mgmt:
92:   - network: oob
93:     ipv4-subnet: 172.31.10.0/24
94:   - network: lab
95:     ipv4-subnet: 172.31.20.0/24
96:     bridge: br-lab
97: topology:
98:   nodes:
99:     r1:
100:       kind: linux
101:       image: alpine
102:       mgmt-net: oob
103:   links:
104:     - type: mgmt-net
105:       network: lab
106:       endpoint:
107:         node: r1
108:         interface: eth1
109:       host-interface: r1-eth1
110: ```
111: 
112: Without `network`, the link keeps attaching to the first management network, preserving existing behavior. Unknown networks and networks not using the bridge driver are rejected.
113: 
114: Done in #3495 thanks to @FloSch62
115: 
116: ## MikroTik CHR improvements
117: 
118: ### Up to 64 data interfaces on AMD64
119: 
120: The [`mikrotik_chr`](../manual/kinds/mikrotik_chr.md) kind places data NICs behind PCI bridges on AMD64, raising the supported interface limit from `ether29` to `ether64` while preserving interface numbering and MAC addresses. ARM64 continues to support up to `ether30`.
121: 
122: At high port counts, the launcher verifies that RouterOS discovered every data NIC and re-plugs missing NICs before reporting the node healthy. It repeats the check after RouterOS reboots itself, so interfaces are recovered after an in-router reboot as well. Interface limits are checked during deployment, with errors naming the offending topology interface.
123: 
124: Done in #3521 and #3568, thanks to @tomashoryl
125: 
126: ### Native management VRF and safer saves
127: 
128: CHR management `ether1` now lives in a native `mgmt` VRF, while data interfaces remain in `main`. SSH, HTTP/HTTPS, API, Winbox, Telnet and SNMP are bound to the management VRF, so lab routes no longer take management replies away from `ether1`. LLDP, CDP and MNDP are disabled on management `ether1` by default while remaining enabled on data interfaces; a startup configuration can select another discovery policy.
129: 
130: Repeated configuration saves now omit an invalid empty default keymat-provider entry emitted by recent RouterOS versions, preserve intentional discovery and service VRF settings, and filter launcher-owned management configuration more reliably.
131: 
132: /// warning | Recreate nodes using the previous management setup
133: The native VRF setup requires a fresh node container. Export the user configuration before recreating an existing node that uses the previous policy-routing setup; the launcher does not migrate it in place.
134: ///
135: 
136: Done in #3545, #3546 and #3547, thanks to @mischadiehm
137: 
138: ## SR Linux links survive chassis reboots
139: 
140: A reboot issued from within an [SR Linux](../manual/kinds/srl.md#reboot) node, such as `tools platform chassis reboot`, now brings the node back with its links, saved configuration and management address intact.
141: 
142: Each `nokia_srlinux` node uses an internal `<node-name>-netns` holder container for its network namespace. The holder is hidden from `inspect` unless `--all` is set and is removed with the node. SR Linux nodes now default to the `unless-stopped` restart policy, while an explicitly configured policy is preserved. Nodes with a user-defined `network-mode` keep the previous behavior.
143: 
144: Done in #3567 thanks to @sacckth and @kaelemc
145: 
146: ## Per-node runtime deprecated
147: 
148: The node-level `runtime` parameter is deprecated and will be removed in a future release. Mixing runtimes within a lab is not supported because management networking and lab-wide operations use the lab-wide runtime. Setting `runtime` on a node, group, kind or defaults block still works but emits a deprecation notice.
149: 
150: Use the global `--runtime` flag or `CLAB_RUNTIME` environment variable instead:
151: 
152: ```bash
153: containerlab deploy -t mylab.clab.yml --runtime podman
154: ```
155: 
156: Done in #3527 thanks to @FloSch62
157: 
158: ## Miscellaneous
159: 
160: - `tools snapshot save` now snapshots the deployed nodes of a partially built lab instead of failing on nodes whose containers were not created. [#3537](https://github.com/srl-labs/containerlab/pull/3537)
161: - Ciena SAOS nodes now stage a topology-supplied license file—provided as a path, URL or embedded blob—into `/config/license` and expose it to the vrnetlab launcher through `SAOS_LICENSE_PATH`. [#3535](https://github.com/srl-labs/containerlab/pull/3535)
162: - SR-SIM finds its running instance using the full process command line, fixing discovery when FEX emulates SR OS on ARM hosts. [#3538](https://github.com/srl-labs/containerlab/pull/3538)
163: - `containerlab events` no longer misses interfaces created while a watcher starts. Failed netlink subscriptions are retried and re-established, with changes during the outage reconciled instead of silently ending interface reporting. [#3532](https://github.com/srl-labs/containerlab/pull/3532)
164: - FRR keeps `vtysh` available throughout installation of the configuration-save wrapper, closing a startup race that could prevent the initial configuration from loading. [#3555](https://github.com/srl-labs/containerlab/pull/3555)
165: - Cisco IOL now propagates a `mgmt-intf` override to the VM launcher when a non-default management interface is selected. [#3557](https://github.com/srl-labs/containerlab/pull/3557)
166: - Destroy and reconfigure operations reliably remove `vxlan-stitch` host interfaces by waiting for node deletion before host-link cleanup, including partial labs with no remaining containers. [#3571](https://github.com/srl-labs/containerlab/pull/3571)
167: - Open vSwitch bridges no longer accumulate stale auto-generated `clab-*` ports across deploy and redeploy operations when endpoint names require sanitized Linux interface names. Destroy also cleans up orphaned generated ports, and `ovs-vsctl` cleanup calls time out instead of hanging when `ovs-vswitchd` is unavailable. [#3573](https://github.com/srl-labs/containerlab/pull/3573)
168: - Empty node, kind and defaults definitions are handled correctly: nodes without a body inherit kind, group and configuration values, while empty kind or defaults blocks no longer cause panics. [#3518](https://github.com/srl-labs/containerlab/pull/3518)
169: - Docker and Podman resolve `network-mode: container:` targets correctly when a lab name contains the node name, fixing deployments of SR Linux nodes with their new network-namespace holders. [#3577](https://github.com/srl-labs/containerlab/pull/3577)
170: - The go-jose dependency was updated to 4.1.8. [#3569](https://github.com/srl-labs/containerlab/pull/3569)
171: - Documentation was clarified for [OcNOS image requirements](../manual/kinds/ipinfusion-ocnos.md), HwSKU-dependent [Dell SONiC interface names](../manual/kinds/dell_sonic.md), and [SR Linux platform licensing](../manual/kinds/srl.md#license). [#3552](https://github.com/srl-labs/containerlab/pull/3552) [#3553](https://github.com/srl-labs/containerlab/pull/3553) [#3575](https://github.com/srl-labs/containerlab/pull/3575)

(End of file - total 171 lines)
</content>

These APIs let services such as clab-api-server provide node access while Kubernetes RBAC continues to authorize each user.\n82: \n83: Done in #3540 thanks to @FloSch62\n84: \n85: ## Selecting the management network of `mgmt-net` links\n86: \n87: With [multiple management networks](../manual/network.md#multiple-management-networks), an extended [`mgmt-net` link](../manual/network.md#additional-connections-to-management-network) can now select its attachment network with the `network` key:\n88: \n89: ```yaml\n90: name: multi\n91: mgmt:\n92:   - network: oob\n93:     ipv4-subnet: 172.31.10.0/24\n94:   - network: lab\n95:     ipv4-subnet: 172.31.20.0/24\n96:     bridge: br-lab\n97: topology:\n98:   nodes:\n99:     r1:\n100:       kind: linux\n101:       image: alpine\n102:       mgmt-net: oob\n103:   links:\n104:     - type: mgmt-net\n105:       network: lab\n106:       endpoint:\n107:         node: r1\n108:         interface: eth1\n109:       host-interface: r1-eth1\n110: ```\n111: \n112: Without `network`, the link keeps attaching to the first management network, preserving existing behavior. Unknown networks and networks not using the bridge driver are rejected.\n113: \n114: Done in #3495 thanks to @FloSch62\n115: \n116: ## MikroTik CHR improvements\n117: \n118: ### Up to 64 data interfaces on AMD64\n119: \n120: The [`mikrotik_chr`](../manual/kinds/mikrotik_chr.md) kind places data NICs behind PCI bridges on AMD64, raising the supported interface limit from `ether29` to `ether64` while preserving interface numbering and MAC addresses. ARM64 continues to support up to `ether30`.\n121: \n122: At high port counts, the launcher verifies that RouterOS discovered every data NIC and re-plugs missing NICs before reporting the node healthy. It repeats the check after RouterOS reboots itself, so interfaces are recovered after an in-router reboot as well. Interface limits are checked during deployment, with errors naming the offending topology interface.\n123: \n124: Done in #3521 and #3568, thanks to @tomashoryl\n125: \n126: ### Native management VRF and safer saves\n127: \n128: CHR management `ether1` now lives in a native `mgmt` VRF, while data interfaces remain in `main`. SSH, HTTP/HTTPS, API, Winbox, Telnet and SNMP are bound to the management VRF, so lab routes no longer take management replies away from `ether1`. LLDP, CDP and MNDP are disabled on management `ether1` by default while remaining enabled on data interfaces; a startup configuration can select another discovery policy.\n129: \n130: Repeated configuration saves now omit an invalid empty default keymat-provider entry emitted by recent RouterOS versions, preserve intentional discovery and service VRF settings, and filter launcher-owned management configuration more reliably.\n131: \n132: /// warning | Recreate nodes using the previous management setup\n133: The native VRF setup requires a fresh node container. Export the user configuration before recreating an existing node that uses the previous policy-routing setup; the launcher does not migrate it in place.\n134: ///\n135: \n136: Done in #3545, #3546 and #3547, thanks to @mischadiehm\n137: \n138: ## SR Linux links survive chassis reboots\n139: \n140: A reboot issued from within an [SR Linux](../manual/kinds/srl.md#reboot) node, such as `tools platform chassis reboot`, now brings the node back with its links, saved configuration and management address intact.\n141: \n142: Each `nokia_srlinux` node uses an internal `<node-name>-netns` holder container for its network namespace. The holder is hidden from `inspect` unless `--all` is set and is removed with the node. SR Linux nodes now default to the `unless-stopped` restart policy, while an explicitly configured policy is preserved. Nodes with a user-defined `network-mode` keep the previous behavior.\n143: \n144: Done in #3567 thanks to @sacckth and @kaelemc\n145: \n146: ## Per-node runtime deprecated\n147: \n148: The node-level `runtime` parameter is deprecated and will be removed in a future release. Mixing runtimes within a lab is not supported because management networking and lab-wide operations use the lab-wide runtime. Setting `runtime` on a node, group, kind or defaults block still works but emits a deprecation notice.\n149: \n150: Use the global `--runtime` flag or `CLAB_RUNTIME` environment variable instead:\n151: \n152: ```bash\n153: containerlab deploy -t mylab.clab.yml --runtime podman\n154: ```\n155: \n156: Done in #3527 thanks to @FloSch62\n157: \n158: ## Miscellaneous\n159: \n160: - `tools snapshot save` now snapshots the deployed nodes of a partially built lab instead of failing on nodes whose containers were not created.
